894.5017/4-649
Gentlemen:
We are enclosing a copy of a letter received from
our agent, Mr. Peroy Breen. We have eliminated the
names of the manufacturers quoted in the original letter
as the manufacturer who had made us this off feels
that he might be subject to reprisal from Boki Cho.
894.602
I cannot believe that it is the polioy of the State
Department to pormit a monopoly. It is also my under-
standing that the U. S. Customs valuations are based on
freely offered merchandise in the regular channels of
trade.
This definitely would be a violation.
Our agent is prepared to quote names to the proper
authority if given the opportunity providing he can be
assured there would be no chance of reprisal against
FILED
the people he may disclose.
5
As we are not sure whother or not the policy ig
established by the State Department or by the Suprent
Command we are sending a copy of this lotter to the
Stato Department in Washington.

=== Page 2 === (confidence 0.99)
Percy Breen
PO Box 494
Tokio Japan
28 February 1949
Park Instrument Company,
Bergenfield, N. J.
Ro: 7 x 50 Binoculars
Your letter A-109
500 pieces
Gentlemen:
I am able to secure this item ( in a quality which appears to
me equal to or superior to the binoculars previously shipped to
you) at $16.50, with coated lens and shoulder straps but without
carrying case.
Before cabling you this information seeking your confirmation
I had the supplier check with Boeki Cho to secure their understand-
ing that a contract on this basis will be validated. The manu-
facturer has just reported back to me tat Boeki Cho will definite-
ly not accept any contract for 7 x 50s priced less than $21.00,
coated lens, shoulder straps and no carrying case.
The manufacturer assures me that in offering this binocular
at $16.50 a fair margin of profit is incl uded. The docision of
Boeki Cho is therefore most disturbing.
The way I see it is that Boeki Cho in establishing the floor
price on this item has been swayed by the opinion of the larger
manufacturers who are determined to uphold their present solling
prices irrespective of their loss of sales arising from German
and French competition enturing the American market. They proba-
bly feel that if the less known brands could be pogged at a price
not less than $2l.00 then they, with their better known brands
and superior quality can outsell the smaller manufacturers even
though their prices be a few dollars hiher.
The above opinion is not substantiated by known facts but is
my private opinion based on conversations I have picked up here
and there. I was told that the large manufacturer was quite con-
fident that importers in the United States would order their binocu-
lars even at $28.00 and, in fact, did receive an order at this
price but the letter of credit to cover never arrived. Local
suppliers who were unable to secure binoculars from the larger
manufacturers can now do so readily with assurance of immediate or
prompt delivery from which fact one can gather the way the wind is
now blowing for the products of this large manufacturer.
If, therefore, my feeling that Boeki Cho's floor price ruling
was determined on the policy of a few select manufacturers is
warranted it is a most disturbing thing. On one hand we have a

=== Page 3 === (confidence 0.99)
manufacturer who, probably due to wise management and satisfied
not with a desire to make a fortune out of one order but with a
legitimate margin of profit, cannot or rather is prohibited
from helping in the economical rehabilitation program through
increased export by Boeki Cho's restrictive measure and on the
other hand a manufacturer with an enormous factory and staff ac-
quired through war time activities for the Japanese armed forees
and disproportionate with current demand.
As long as Boeki Cho prohibits legitimate, free compoition
on 7 x 50s no effort on my part will avail in securing this item
for you competitively.
Very truly y ours,
PERCY BREEN

=== Page 4 === (confidence 0.95)
MAY 271949
In reply refer to
C?
My dear Mr. Sandera:
This will acknowledge the receipt of a copy of your letter
of April 6, 1949 to the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers
894.5017/4-549
protesting the placing of floor prices on Japanese binoculars.
I am sorry for the delay in acknowledging it.
You may already have received a reply from the Supreme
Commander who is, of course, in a better position than we are to
disouss the aspects of your ase. It is our understanding, however,
that the ocoupation authoritis have no intention of encouraging
the growth of monopolies in J pan. The setting of floor prices on
exports is presumably aimed at maximizing the returns from exports.
There is also a desire to avoid a development of unfair Japanese
competition.
Thank you for your courtesy in sending to the Department a
copy of your letter. e will be interested in the reply which the
occupation authorities send to you.
Sincerely yours,
m
Merrill C. Gay
Assistant Chief
ivision of Commercial olicy
Mr. Samuel Sanders,
ark Instrument Company,
New Bridge Road,
MA1949
Bergenfield, New Jeraey.
Bir
May 23, 1949
N.+

My dear Mr. Jobsis:
This is in roply to your letter of May 3, 1949 trans-
mitting a copy of the enamelware price list of the "Associa-
tion des Emailleries Europeennes" (No. la), a list showing
a comparison betwoen the official f.o.b. prices of the

INTRODUCTION
In April, 1947, the Far Eastern Contthission mado an announoement to the effoct that the 
neods of the Japanese pooplo should be substantiadly tho standard of living provailing in Japan
the period of 1930-34: Presumably tho futuro scale of industries to be allowed to Japan
determined also on that basis. Then, what was the Japanose standard of living in 1930-84?
In Auguet 1947, tho Political Section of the Gencral Affairs Bureau, Minietry of Foreign, Affaira,
sot up an informal committee for tho purpose of studying the subjoct from a broad and comprohensive
viewpoint. This committee, with the help and cooperation of various Governmental and privat
agoncies, and after more than two months' work, gathered voluminous data, of which the present
report is a summary.

CHAPTER I.
POPULATION IN 1930-34
1. The population of Japan Proper during 1930-34, which averaged 66,339,000 as shown in Table
1, increased year after year, the index for the year 1934 being 105.9, which compares with 100 for
the year 1930 when the total population was 64,450,000. The average annual increase was 950,500
Of the average total population for 1930-34, 50.2 % was male and 49.8%, femalo.
2. When divided into the productive popuntion (15-59 years) and the pon-productive population
(below 14 aul over 60), the percentago of the productive population shows a tendeney to decrease, though
slightly, from 56.0 % for 1930 to 55.5 % for 1934. The average ratio between the two ago groups for
1930-34 was 55.8 % for the productive population to 44.2 % for the non-productive. The latter in its
turn divides iteelf into the below-proluctive age group (36.8 %), and the above-productive age group
(7.4 %).
3. The ratios between males and females respeetively within the productive and non-productive
populations are shown in Tables 3-5. Taken on the average during 1930-34, the pereentage of males
among the tender-aged is 50.4 % but in the case of the productive population, it increases to 50.8%.
Among the above-productive age group, the males decrease to 44.6 %, while the females inerease to
55.4 %, indicating the longevity of women.
4. In summarizing the above, we get the Japan's population composition in 1930-34. (v. Table 6.)
CHAITER II.
OCCUPATIONAL DISTRIBUTION (OF THE POPULATION)
I. Employment
1. Population employed in various industrial groups (Part. 1)
What kind of a productive life this population of 66,339,000, 1930-34 average, was leading?
Table 7 shows the population employed in various occupational groups during, the period of 1930-34.
Averaging these tive years, the greatest portion of the population was employed in agriculture and
forestry, which formed 47.8 % of the total employed population. Following them were industry with
20.2 %; conmerce with 15.9 %: publie service and liberal professions, 6.2 %; transportation, 4.0 %;
and others, 5.4 %.
Again, Table 8 shows comparative inereases and decreases of population employed in various
occupations during 1930-34. In the total number of employed population, the figures for 1934 show
a net increase of 1.174.000 over those of 1930, and if we represent the tigures of the latter year by
100, the index for the former will be 105.5. Among the industrial groups, the one which showed the
greatest increase is manufaeturing industry with the index figures of 111.4. Next comes mining with
109.8, followed by publie service and liberal professions with 109.6. The smallest figures are 100.3 for
agriculture.
If we represent the total number of increase in the employed population, 1,174,000, as 100%,
and count the perecentage of inereases in various occupational groups, industry shows the greatest
increase with 65.7 %, followed by publie service and liberal professions with 14.4 %. The other groups
constitute small proportions-none excceding 5 %. In agricultural communities, as is seen from Table
9, the natural inerease of population was very high, showing a percentage three times larger than in
the urban armas. However, the index of total agricultural population for 1934 was only 100.3, (with
the figure for 1930 as 100), less by 5.2 compared with 105.5, the index figures for the total employed

population, the increase in the former was only 3.9 % of the total increaso in the latter.
(Note) In Tables 7 and 8, showing the employed populstion in various industrial group no mentiom
is msde of the : nemployed. This is becse the census of 1930, and also the estimates made by the

2. Population employed in various industrial groups (Part 2)
If we divide the employed population into three groups—(1) those employed in the primary (i. a.
agriculture, forestry and fishery), (2) the secondary (i. e. mining and manufacturing industay) and
(3) the tertiary (i. e. commerce, transportation, publie service and liberal professions, domostic sorvieo
and others) classes, the result is as shown in Table 11. According to this, the population employed in
the primary class formed 49.7 % of the total employed population in 1930. It inereased to 51.0% in
1931, and decreased yearly after 1932. In 1931, when the pereentage of the population in tho
primary class showed a marked inerease, the secondary class decreased to 20.0% from 21.0% of the
previous year, and in the like manner, the tertiary class also decreased to 29.0% from 29.3%. This
may be attributed to the fact that those thrown out from their jobs in the secondary, and aleo in the
tertiary, classes, as a consequence of the wave of economic depression which visited this country
around 1930 and 1931, were mostly absorbed into the agricultural population by virtue of the special
social structure existed in Japan. After 1932, the population in the secondary class inereased rapidly—
from 20.0% of 1931 to 20.6% of 1932, 21.5% of 1933 and 22 8% of 1933. In the like manner,
the tertiary chss population rose also from 28.6% of 1932 to 28.9% of 1933 and 29.3% of 1934.
That such inereases in the secondary and the tertiary classes were mainly dte to the influx of
workers from the agrieultural areas, is indicated by the fact that the population employed in the
primary industries, after reaching its peak in 1931 with the ratio of 51.0%, fell ofl markedly to
50.8% (1932), 49.6% (1933) and 47.9% (1934).
Thus, the average ratios for 1930-34 of the population employed in the three classes of industries
are as follows:

Now, if we classify the employed population under the categories of (1) consumer goods industries
(i. e. agriculture, forestry, fishery and consumptive goods industries). (2) productive goods industries
(i. e. mining and producer goods industries) and (3) service group (i. e. commerce, transportation,
publie service and liberal professions, domestie service and other industries), the result is as shown in
Table 12. Averaging 1930-34, the population etployed in the consumer goods industries formed
60.1%, the producer goods industries, 10.9%, and the service group, 29.0%, respectively of the total
number. This means that in the total employed population of every 100 persons, approximately 60
were oupied for producing the necessaries of life, while about 11 were engaged in manufacturing the
producer goods needed for producing such necessaries, and about 29 were engaged in the servico
group. The faet that the average employment ratio for 1930-34 was 44.7%, signifies that every 100
employed persons supported 223 persons ineluding themselves.

In 1931, employment in the consumer goods industries showed an increase of 0.9% compared to

4 Fluctuation of population in various industrial groups
As for-the total employed population in 1934, there were 105.5 persons for every 100 in 1980, the
actual number of the inerease being 1,174,000. Of this increase, industry took up the greatest portion,
or 65.5%, with a total figure of 771,000 persons. Table 15 shows the fluctuation of population in
various industrial groups during 1930-34.
Of the total increase in the industrial population—771,000—the groups which accounted for the
greatest numbers were machine and tools industry and metal industry with 30.6% and 29.2%
respectively. Next came textiles (20.5%), chemienls (12.5%) anl other industries (14.7%). On the
other hand. those registering decreases were foodstuffs (-8.8%) and gas, electricity and water
industries (-1.6%). The other groups showed no material change during the period.
The notable features to be noted in employment composition of the 1930-34 period may bo
summarized as follows:
(1) An inereasing movement of the agricultural population into urban industries.
Approximately 75% of the total natural inerease of population in this period were reported from
the rural districts, but the increase of the employed population in agriculture and forestry was only
3.9%, most of the rest, 65.6%, being absorbed in industry.
(2) Difference in the rate of population absorption among various industrial groups.
The rate of aborbing population differs among the various industrial groups. The machine and
tools and the metal industries which may be termed as " modern industries" stand highest, followed
by the textiles and the chemicals industries, whereas the foodstuffs industry shows a decrease.
(3) Noticeable inerease in the population employed in the secondary class.
In the changing employment composition of various industrial groups, a decrease in the primary
class (agriculture, forestry and fishery), a marked increase in the secondary class (mining and
industry), and a gradual inereàse in the tertiary (service) class are to be noted.
Mr. Colin Cark, in his " Conditions of Economic Progress" (published ip 1941), arrives at tho

=== Page 25 === (confidence 0.98)
一.8-
conclusion, after studying the national incomes of varions natione, that 
economically and its national inoome increascs, ite population decreases proportionatel
class, while increasipg in the secondary clas; and as its economies advance further, the
population of the socondaty class stops, and the tertiary class begins to gain. May it not be
the same tendency is descernible in the population composition of Japan during 1980-34?
II. Productive Activities
In what kind of productive aetivities this population, whose employment composition we have jut
examined, was being engaged? The donmestic production of principal goods in quantities during 1880 4
is shown in Table 16. According to this, the average production of rico during the period 
62,574,000 koku. The figure for 1934 was over 70,829,000 koku, which was the greatest crop in the
twenty years since 1926. The average production of coal during the period was 31,173,000 tons, or
about 55% of the peak figure of 56.312,000 tons in 1910. As for iron and steel, production averaged
2,200,000 tons for ordinary steel, 1,250,000 tons for pig-iron and 2,360,000 tons for steel, being 45%
30%, and 43% respectively of the pak production of the war years. The production of cotton yam
was 1,157,965,000 pounds, or about 73% of 1937, the peak year in the twenty years since 1926. The
figures for many other items fell considerably below the period after 1935. As an illustration, Table 17
shows the operation conditions of the cotton yarn and tissue industry during 1930-34 abd adjoining
years.
The average demand and supply during 1930-34 of prineipal goods thus produced were as shown
in Table 18. According to this, the total domestie consumption of rice was 73,360,000 koku, and per
capita consumption 1.105 koku; the total coal consumption, 33,073,000 tons, and. per capita
consumption, 0.499 tons; and the total consumption of cotton yarn, 415,073,900 pounds, and-par
capita consumption 6.26 pounds. As for iron and steel, the tigures for the total domestic consumption
of ordinary steel, pig iron and steel were 2,230,000 tons, 1,900,000 tons and 2,360,000 tons respectively,
while per capita consumption figures were 33.7 kilograns, 28.6 kilograms and 35.6 kilograms
respectively.
The total gross production and net production tigures for the various industries are shown in Table
19 in prices. A net production value is the balance after deducting the pecessary material costs and
power and fuel costs from the gross production. Tables 20 and 21 show the gross production, materinl
costs, power costs and net produetion of manufacturing industries and mining.
Table 22 shows the annual net production tigures for manufacturing industries âuring 1930-34.
We find that of the average total increase in the gross production during the period, metal industry
accounted for the greatest percentage of 28.4%, followed by 26.9% of the textile, 17.9% of the
chemieal and 14.1% of the machine and tools. A marked expansion was achieved by the heavy and
chemieal industries. while it is to be noted that the textile industry was still showing a big increase —
in fact, the second largest.
However, from the figures for the yearly gross production of the various manufacturing industries
in Table 23, we find that the textile industry began to decline after 1931, while the growth of the
industries such as metal, machine and tools and chemical was steadily accelerated.
With the increase of relative importanee of heavy and chemical industries, the proportion of big
sized factories increased also. As shown in Table 24, the proportion of factories enmploying more than

III. Foreign Trade Activities
The fluctuations of domestie prices — the wholesale, retail and general prices — from 1926 onward,
with the figures for 1930-34 average as the basic 100, are shown in Table 30. In this, it will be
noted that the lowest points in all three price indices were reached in 1931. Before 1931, tho declime
in pricest first began in the retail prices, but the rate of decline was greater in the wholesale prices and
in the bottom year of 1931 the latter were at a lower level than the former. However, when the
a
retail prices gradually picked up from 1931, the wholesale prices also rose at a higher rate. In 1937,
the index for the wholesale prices wás 137.s, about 18% higher than that of the retail prices which
was 119.8. After 1937, the retail prices index always advanced at a higher rate than that of the
wholesale prices, reflecting the abmormal cconomic condition of war-time.
The price fluctuations in Japan, as compatred with Great Britain and the United States for the
period of 1929-34, are shown in Table 31, according to which, although the domestie wholesalo
prices showed some gain over 1930, the rate of decline in dollar and sterling exchanges was greater.
Conseqnently, in terms of dollars Japanese prices were lowe than the prices in the United States and
Grent Britain, a cireunstance which contributed to the expansion of Japan's export trade during that
period. Table 33 shows the relationship between the depreciation of foreign exehange and tho
expansion of foreign trade. However, it must be borne in mind that such expansion in foreign trade
was effected under the conditions of comparative ris in import prices and decline in export prices as
is shown in Table 33, which means deterioration in the foreign trade conditions.
The developntent of foreign trade brought about the expansion of commodity production (i. e.

and export) to the total commodity production rose gradually from the lowest figure (22.0%) for 1931

Table 61 shows the international balance of 1930-34 as a whole, by sunming up trade b

VI. National Income
By putting together the net production of various industries (in other words, the produotive
national income) listed in Table 20 and the international balance of Table 61 (with payment surph
as +), and adding to them the transfer ineomes arising from public finance, the indirect taxes, the
depreciation fund accounts, the apprecintion of stocks, inventory checking and other adjustment
items. we can estimate the national ineomes of 1930-34, which are listed in Table 62.
According to this list, the average nationa! income (or disposable income) of 1930-34 was about
14,000 million yen, of which 12,285 million yen represented the productive national income. Increae
and decreases in the productive national income during 1930-34 are•shown in Table 63.' Compared
with 1930, 1934 showed an increase of 2,771 million yen in productive national income.
As regards the percentages of various industrial groups in the inerease of productive national
income, industry ranked the highest with 55.8%. The fact that the manufacturing industries apeounted
for 48.4% of the total increase, shows how great was the rate of their expansion in those years. Next
comes commerce with 15.7%, followed by agriculture and forestry with 12.2%. Although agriculture
and forestry accounted for 15.5% of the total productive national income, their share in its increase
was only 12.2%, signifying a falling of agriculture and forestry, as compared with other groups.
As has been mentioned elsewhere, Table 29 shows per capita productive national income (or net
production) of various industrial groups. The figures for agriculture and forestry inereased from 133.3
yen for 1930 to 156.4 yen for 1934, a net mcrease of mere 23.1 yen. Compared with the similar
increase in industry — from 591.1 yen for 1930 to 734.9 yen for 1934, an inerease of 143.8 yen —it
was only one-sixth of the latter.
The net increase in mining was only 102 million yen, but the rate of increase was almost 40%,
next only to industry. The other industrial groups showed increase rates of around 10%.
Thus, the inerease in the national ineome during the period of 1930-34 was derived wholely from
the increase in the productive national ineome, which in turn owed 59.5% to the increase in industry
and mining.
CHAPTER III.
CONSUMPTIVE ACTIVITIES
The consumptive activities of the pation are made possible by the national ineome acquired through
the productive activities. The yearly national ineome is renewed by replenishing the capital consumed
for production during the preceding year, and also by supplying new capital for expansion. In
examining the distribution of national income, therefore, it is neccessary to divide it into two parts—
national consumption and national investment.

CHAPTER IV.
COMPARISON OF THE STANDARD OF LIVING
1. Estimation of standard of living
Inasmuch as a nation's consumptive life comprises services as well as material consumption, both
of these factors must be taken into consideration in estinating the standard of living of a nation.
2. Comparison of Japnese standard of living through years
(1). The yearly comparison of the per capita real national ineomes.
Table S6 shows the per capita real national ineomes (which means national ineomes divided by
the price index for cach year) from 1926 to 1947, with the general price index of 1930-34 average
set as the basic standard (v. Chart 1). It is to le noted that the 1930-34 average per capita real
national ineomt of 165 yen is sonewhat higher than in the years preceding 1931, but lower than in
the years after 1933. The post-surrender years of 1946 and 1947 showed the figures of 85 yen and
76 yen respéctively, about 50% of the 1930-34 average figures.
(2) Comparison by the revised Bennett methoL
The Hypothee Bank of Japan made the yearly comparison of Japan's standard of living by
revising the method used by Mr. Bennett in his estinate of the levels of the living standard of
various nations. This method is followed in Table 87 and Clart 2. Although there may be some
doubts about the accuracy of the Bennett method, we introduce it bere for referenee. According to
this, the standard of living of 1930-34 average was low compared with the years 1927, 1928 and 1929,
as well as with the years after 1934. It rose yearly after 1926, but in 1930 it began to fall rapidly,
and renched its bottom in 1932. However, it began to rise again after 1933 and reached its peak in
1935, just before the war years intervened.
3. International Comparison
It is very difficult to make an international comparison of the standard of living inasmuch as the
purchasing power of money as well as the node and manners of living are different among the
nations. However, we cite here some of the representative estimations so far made on this subject.
(1) Comparison of national incomes of various nations mayle by the Cabinet Statisties Bureau.
The Cabinet Statisties Bureau made a comparison of national incomes of various nations in terms
of yen at the prevailing exchange rates for the respective years (Table 88). Though the data picked
are not of the same year, Japan's per capita national inconne for 1930 was about one-ninth of that of
U. S. A., one-eighth of Great Britain, one-fifth of France and one-half of Belgium.
(2) Comparison made by Mr. Collin Clark.

=== Page 33 === (confidence 0.97)
Aocorling to Mr. Colin Clark's " Conditions of Eoonomio Poogrom" (1940),
income wae 358 international anite as is shown in Table 89.
Note: An international unit is the amount of goods and services to be pogel
in the United States, 1925-34 average.
Compared with other nations' real national ineontés, although Japan stands far below manb
as U. S. A., Canada, Great Britain etc., all showing more than 1,000 intermational unita,
with such nations as Finland, Hungary, Poland, Italy otc.
(3) Comparison by the Bennett method.
Mr. M. K. Bennett published an article entitled "On Compariaon of the Standard of Living of Natlone
in the February, 1937 issue of the "Quarterly Journal of Economics", in which be made an
international comparison of living standards. In his study, Japan was not included; but if we maks
an estimate of Japan's standard of living according to his method, and put it into his chart, the
result is as shown in Tables 90 and 91. The resuit is similar to Mr. Colin Clark's study, and Japan'
standard of living is on the similar leve! with Finland and Italy.

at 1930-34 1evels. Assuming a population increase by 1953 of 26 per

tion during the base period, the study concludes that an increase of
20 per cent cver the 1930-34 average will be required in the national
income. Assuning further that the elements from which this income is
derived grow in the same relative proportion as they did between
1930 and 1934 , the :entribution of industry to the new and enlarged
naticnal income will require a 46 per cent increase in industrial
prcduction. the study continues, since it is not posaible to expect
Large increases in agriculture, forestry, mining, and the cther non-
industrial categories.

Enclosure No. 1 to Despatch No.
801 dated November 17, 1949 from
the United States Politioal Ad-
viser for Japan, Tokyo, on the
RESTRIGTED
subject "Transmission of Copies
of Memoranda Exohanged between
United Kingdom Liaison Mission
in Japan and General Headquar-
ters, SCAP, regarding the Eli-
mination of Floor Prices in
Japan".
(COPY)
United Kingdom Liaison Mission in Japan,
British Embassy,
Tokyo.
EA 29/411/49
7th November, 1949.
My dear General,
You will doubtless have read in the press of the
commotion caused in the United Kingdom by the aboli-
tion of Japanese floor prices.
2. I am sure you will appreciate the political
difficulties with which His Majesty's Government are
:onfronted. There were top questions for answer in
the House of Commons on this subjeot last week: The
Times had a significant leader on October 3lst whfch
(I gather) voiced the fears felt in United Kingdom
trade and industrial circles as a result of bitter
pre-war experiences; the Chairman of the Cotton Board
has made representations to the President of the Board
of Trade.
3. His Majesty's Government greatly welcome the
safeguards already announced against possible unfair
Japanese trade competition arising from this recent
development and have noted in particular that General
Headquarters have instruc ted the Japanese Government
to accord full consideration to the following general
principles:-
(a) Export prices should not be less than the
prices of like commodities of comparable
grade and quality when offered for sale
on the domestic market to corresponding
types of trade.
Major General W. F. Marquat,
Economic and Scientific Section,
General Headquarters,
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers.
(b) For those
RESTRTCTED

=== Page 318 === (confidence 0.99)
Inclsoure No. 1 to Tokyo's
Despatch No. 8ol dated
N:vember 17, 1949.
RESTRIOTED
-2-
(b)
For those commodities or grades of commodi-
ties pi duced solely for export and which
thererore do not have domestic prices, the
expor:  rices should not be less than the
total  at of production plus necessary
handling charges and reasonable profit.
4. SCAP could greatly help His Majesty's Govern-
ment in the latter's opinion, further to explain and
justify his policy of removing control on Japanese
export prices if he felt able to issue a rather fuller
and more significant statement about his instrustions
to the Japanese Government than has been issued up to
date.
5. There are two pointa in particular on which
it would be very timely, in London's opinion. to obtain
publi: assurances from the Supreme Comaander:-
First, as both the United Kingdom wool and textile
machinery pricing missions to Japan have pointed out,
Japanese exporters often quote quite unnecessarily low
pri es, thereby spoiling the market for themselves as
well as for their competitors. This is as undesirable
fron the pcint of view of Japanese economy as it is
from the British, since it leads to an unnecessary
losa of foreign exchange.
Serondly, there is the demoralization of markets
produced by the pre-war Japanese practice of frequently
lowering quotations for the same article. This causes
buyers to hcld off in the expectation of lower prices
later and makes them especially careful about their
forward commitments even for goods from other countries,
for the obvious reason that the change in the market
at one end might easily upset the prices at the other.
Reports have recently reached the United Kingdom of
such price hanges.
6. I very much hope that General Headquarters will
see their way to issuing a statement of this nature
(spe ifically forbidding these and similar practices)
which would serve to allay any unnecessary anxieties
caused by the abolition of Japanese floor prices. I am
under instructions to make an approach to you on these
lines as a matter of urgency.
7. A press statement by the Japanese Prime Ninis-
ter expressing the Japanese intention of maintaining
this policy after the peace treaty would naturelly also
have a reassuring effect on world opinion.
Sincerely,
COPY:
Mr. McDermott
E. R. LINGEMAN,
Mr. Pickelle
Minister (Commercial),
Mr. Boehringer
Acting Head of Mission.

383.8(14 NOV 49)ESS/EX
MEMORANDUM FOR: The Head of the United Kingdom Liaison
Mission in Japan
SUBJECT: Elimination of Floor Prices.
i.. This will acknowledge your memorandum dated
7 November 1949, reference EA 29/411/49.
2. The political difficulties confronting His
Majesty:s government are appreciated fully. The pur-
pose of the recent announcement by the Supreme Com-
mander for the Allied Powers concerning the removal
of floor prices was to adjust Japanese international
trade procedures to the pattern of export prioing
generally accepted by other nations.
3. You may be confident that every cssible
effort will be exerted to prevent the use of unfair
and unorthodox methods whenever and wherever they may
appear in Japanese trade negotiations. I would wel-
come any specific information of the existence of
such practices should they develop and your suggestions
for corrective action.
W.F.MARQUAT
Major General, U.S. Army
Chief, Economic and Scientific Section

The Acting Political Adviser has the honor to refer to the
press release issued October 25, 1949, by General Headquarters,
Supreme Conmander for the Allied Powers. regarding the aboli-
tion of all Japanese export floor prices with the exception of
those on raw siik and silk piece goods, the latter being scheduled
for removal on December 31, 1949. Further reference is made to
a ccpy of a memorandum of a conversation dated December 7, 1949,
on the subject of "Abolition of Floor Prices in Japan," giving
an account of a conversation on that date held in the Department
and participated in by r. G. J. JOBSIS, representative of the
Netherlands Embassy, and officers of the Department. According
to this memorandum. a copy of which was transmitted to this
Mission as enclosure to a transmittal note of identic date,
Mr. Jobsis brought up the question as to the type of control
which might be exercised by SCAP and Japanese authorities to pre-
vent dumping of Japanese goods abroad, following abolition of
export floor prices; in this connection, Mr. Jobsis expressed a
desire to see the appropriate SCAPIN or other instructions issued
by Genera Headquarters to the Japanese Government on the aboli-
tion of iloor prices.
It is regretted that copies of pertinent documents involved
in this question were not transmitted to the Department following
the release here and in lashington of the October 25 press re-
lease. It was believed that the subject had been adequately
covered by the press release and that pertinent background infor-
mation would be reported to the concerned Departments in Wash-
ington by their representatives on the Advisory Mission on Inter-
national Trade, of which Ar. Ormond FREILE was Chairman.
In view of the obvious importance of the question raised by
Mr. Jobsis, and also by various American and Eritish trade groups,
regarding control to prevent Japanese dumping, it is appreciated
FAP
that copies of all pertirent documents involved in the abolition
of export floor prices are of considerable importance. Accor-
dingly, there is now enclosed a copy of a memorandum dated

October 6, 1949, from the Linistry of International Trade and
Industry (LITI) to the Foreign Trade and Commerce Division, Eco-
nomic and Scientific Section (Ess), entitled "Plan to Safeguard
Export Against Probable Dangers Arising from Abolition of Floor
Price System", which is the basic document on this subject. It
will be noted that the enclosed MITI memorandum makes reference
to a previous conumunication from Ess dated September 16, 1949.
The latter was merely a refusal on the part of ESs to consides
a similar but incomplete plan submitted by MITI at that time,
and concluded with an ESs suggestion that a more detailad study
be submitted for further consideration.
The MITI memorandum of Cctober 6, 1949, encloses a draft
instruction to LTmt branch offices in implementation of the abo-
lition of floor prices
ishing a procedu for checking
the prices quoted in export license applications wnien are to
be referred to ITI itself for ultimate decision if any discre-
pancy is noted between export and domestic prices. kITI states
that the close surveillance esta lished in its draft instruction
to branch offices, and the careful checking of all export cargo
by Customs officials, are the only actual safeguards to be insti-
tuted against possible dunping practices. It points out. however,
that lack of information on werld prices and the shortage of expe-
rienced personnel might well lessen the efficie y of thes sfe-
guards and therefore. recommends the following "counter-measures"
to preclude the adoption of punitive or retaliatory measures by
foreign countries against possible Japanese dumping:
1. "Closer contact with government of buying countries"
and "enlightenaent of Japanese makers and suppliers"
with reference to price levels and trade practices which
could be publicized by MITI for the information of Japa-
nese exporters. MITI implies that this coordination
could best be carried out by Japanese representatives
stationed abroad; and
2. "Effective self-restraint of export" which, according
to LITI, can best be exercised by the "export industry
concerned and supported by the Government authorities."
The MITI memorandwn concludes with a statement that, while
the immediate abolition of floor prices in general is favored,
it recommends the publication of a list of specific commodities
for which the floor prices would be left in effect as a "stop-
gap measure". A list of these export com.odities is attached to
the MITI memorandum.
2/
There is also enclosed a emorandum from ESS to kITI of
October 22, 1949. which approves the LITI plan and the draft
instructions to its branch offices provided the Japanese Govern-
ment accords full consideration to the principle that export prices
shall

shall not be less than those of like commodities offered for sale
on the domestic market, or, if such commodities be produced ohly
for export, that the export prices should not be less than the
total cost of production plus necessary handling charges and a
reasonable profit. ESS also charged MITI with the responsibility
for keeping Japanese exporters advised as to appropriate regula-

sponsorship, and also re jected the list of commodities for which

commodities except raw silk and silk piece goods. According to a
SCAP press release of October 25, 1949, issued by the Public Infor-

have been guaranteed up to December 31, 1949, under previous SCAP
commitments, and floor prices on raw silk and silk piece goods
will not be removed until January 1, 1950.
